Down or flat? Yeah... yeah, I'd say that's accurate. It's been a long time. This has been going on since I was pulled back into full operational leadership, which was... let's see, about two years and four months ago now. 

They restructured the whole IT department. Brought in this consulting firm from Chicago—bunch of kids in expensive suits who didn't know a server blade from a toaster. They tried to implement this "agile" framework across the board. Complete disaster. We had perfectly good legacy systems running the inventory databases. I remember when we first installed those IBM mainframes back in the early nineties. Took a crane to get them into the building. We had to reinforce the raised flooring in the data center just to handle the weight. The contractor who did the flooring, Sal, he used to bring in these massive boxes of cannolis from a bakery in the North End. Real ricotta, none of that cheap filler. Sal had this old Ford F-150, must have had three hundred thousand miles on it. The tailgate was held shut with a bungee cord, and he had this golden retriever named Buster who rode shotgun everywhere he went. Buster would just sit there with his head out the window, ears flapping in the wind. 

But, uh... yeah. Ever since that restructure, getting pulled back into the day-to-day operations... it's just been this constant, flat feeling. Most days, yeah.

It all just feels like a checklist now instead of anything that actually brings me joy. \n\nTake the church concert last month, for example. My wife wanted to go to the spring choir recital. Now, the choir director, Mrs. Higgins, she's been running that thing for thirty years. Thirty years! And she flat out refuses to use the new microphones we bought for the sanctuary. I actually helped the church's AV committee pick them out—top of the line, wireless, completely interference-free. But she insists on using this old wired mic from the nineties that crackles every time someone steps on the cord. Drives me absolutely up the wall because I know how much of the church funds went into the new system. We even had a special bake sale for it. My wife made her famous pecan pies, which, let me tell you, are always the very first thing to sell out. She uses this dark corn syrup that just makes all the difference. \n\nBut... what was the question? Oh, feeling down. Right. Yeah, sitting there at the concert, listening to them sing, I just realized I didn't care. I wasn't enjoying the music, I wasn't enjoying being out. It was just another box to check off the list so I could go home. Just gray.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like most of the day, every day, you're just going through the motions emotionally, without really feeling much of anything—or do you feel down or low for most of the day, even if it's not intense?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's pretty much what I was saying before. Most of the day, every day, it's just that same gray, flat feeling. Just going through the motions. Like I said about the tomatoes and the fishing, and even that church concert... it's all just a checklist. \n\nSpeaking of checklists, they actually had me reviewing the server maintenance logs at work yesterday. We just migrated the whole county database to a new cloud-based system, and let me tell you, the vendor promised a seamless transition. Seamless! What a joke. The integration with our legacy billing software completely failed on day one. I had three different department heads calling my desk before 8:00 AM. And the guy from the vendor—what was his name? Chad, I think. Yeah, Chad. He tried to tell me it was an issue with our local network configuration. I've been managing these systems for twenty-five years, I think I know my own network configuration! I actually had to pull up the firewall logs and show him line by line where their API was dropping the connection. \n\nI mean, the whole thing took me four hours to sort out, and I missed lunch. Usually, I'd go down to that little deli on 4th Street—they make this amazing pastrami on rye, real thick-cut, with that spicy brown mustard. The owner, Sal, he always saves me a pickle spear... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, feeling down most of the day. Yeah, it's just like I said. That flat, gray feeling. Most days I'm just checking boxes until I can go to sleep, whether I'm at work dealing with Chad or at home looking at my ruined tomatoes. It just doesn't lift.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way—like you’re just going through the motions emotionally or feeling down—for most days over the past two years, even if it hasn’t always been severe?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I said earlier, it's been pretty consistent since they pulled me back into full operational leadership at the county. That was about two years and four months ago. \n\nAnd you know, speaking of the county, they just sent out this memo about changing our health insurance providers again. This is the third time in five years! They're switching us over to some HMO plan where you need a referral just to get a hangnail looked at. I was looking through the provider directory they sent—which, by the way, was printed in a font so small you need a magnifying glass just to read it—and my dentist isn't even in-network anymore. Dr. Henderson. I've been going to him since my kids were in middle school. He's got this great setup in his waiting room with a huge saltwater aquarium. My youngest used to just sit there and watch the clownfish for hours. He actually ended up getting a degree in marine biology, if you can believe it. Works down in Florida now, studying manatees or something. \n\nAnyway, the insurance thing is a total mess. I tried calling HR to ask about the out-of-network deductible, and I was on hold for forty-five minutes before the system just disconnected me. Forty-five minutes! I could have rewired a server rack in that time. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, the two years thing. Right. Yeah, it's been most days since I took that operational role back on, two years and four months ago. It really hasn't let up since then. Just that same flat feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past couple of years—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask about that, because I was just looking for my brown leather belt the other morning. The one with the brass buckle? I bought it at this little artisan market up in Asheville about ten years ago. We took a trip up there for our anniversary, and let me tell you, the drive through the Blue Ridge Mountains in October is just spectacular. The leaves were at peak color—these vibrant reds and oranges that just make everything look like a painting. We stayed at this quaint little bed and breakfast run by a couple who retired from the airline industry. The husband, Arthur, used to be a pilot for Pan Am back in the day. He had all these incredible stories about flying into Hong Kong before they built the new airport, when you had to basically thread the needle between the skyscrapers to land. \n\nAnyway, I was looking for that belt because I've noticed my pants are getting a bit loose lately. My wife actually pointed it out last week when we were getting ready for church. She said my slacks were hanging off me. And it's not like I'm trying to drop any pounds or anything. I think my appetite's just been... off, I guess, more days than not. Like I was saying about missing lunch when I was dealing with Chad and the server issue—that happens a lot now. I'll just work right through the afternoon and not even realize I haven't eaten. And then when I get home, I just kind of pick at whatever my wife makes for dinner. Even when she makes her pot roast, which used to be my absolute favorite. She slow-cooks it for eight hours with carrots and pearl onions... \n\nBut yeah, what was it? Weight and appetite. Right. So, yeah, I'm definitely eating less, just not really feeling hungry, and I guess I've lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling overly guilty or like you're not worth much lately?\n\nPatient: Well, guilty? No, not really. I mean, I don't feel like I've done anything wrong, or that I'm worthless, if that's what you mean. I've always been a pretty steady provider for my family, you know? Even when things got tight back in '08 when the county had that hiring freeze and they were talking about furloughs. \n\nMan, that was a stressful time. I remember my neighbor, Bill—he worked in construction, mostly residential developments—and his whole company just went under practically overnight. He had this beautiful custom-built pontoon boat sitting in his driveway that he had to sell for pennies on the dollar. I actually thought about buying it myself, you know, for fishing out on the lake. It had this incredible trolling motor and a built-in live well. But my wife said it would just be an eyesore, and honestly, our driveway is on a bit of an incline anyway. It's a pain just getting the riding mower out of the shed without scraping the deck on the concrete. I had to replace the blades twice last summer because of that. \n\nBut, uh, no. I don't feel guilty or anything like that. Just that same flat feeling I mentioned before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have trouble focusing or thinking clearly most days?\n\nPatient: Focusing? No, not really. I mean, I don't think I have trouble thinking clearly. My mind's pretty sharp, especially when it comes to the systems at work. You kind of have to be, you know? \n\nTake last Tuesday, for instance. We were doing this massive migration from our legacy servers over to a new cloud-based infrastructure. They brought in these consultants from some firm in Seattle—bunch of kids in their twenties who probably learned to code before they could ride a bike. One of them, I think his name was Tyler or maybe Taylor, he had this vintage Casio calculator watch on. I haven't seen one of those since the eighties! I actually had one just like it when I first started working at RadioShack back in the day. Man, RadioShack... I used to love the smell of that place. All that fresh solder and electronic components. I bought my first Tandy computer there. Saved up for six months to get it, and it only had, what, 4 kilobytes of RAM? You had to hook it up to a cassette recorder just to save your programs. It took twenty minutes to load a simple text game, and half the time the tape would jam anyway. I remember trying to fix one of those cassette decks with a Q-tip and some rubbing alcohol, and I ended up getting the cotton swab stuck right in the gears. \n\nBut, uh, no, my focus is fine. I can still troubleshoot a network issue faster than most of those young guys they hire. I don't have any trouble thinking clearly. Just... just that flat feeling, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you move or speak more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, that you can't sit still or feel restless most days?\n\nPatient: Move or speak slowly? No, I don't think so. I haven't noticed anything like that. And I'm not really the restless type, either. I've always been pretty even-keeled, you know? Never one to bounce my leg or pace around the room. \n\nActually, my brother-in-law, Dave, he's the restless one. The guy cannot sit still to save his life. We went over to their place for Thanksgiving last year, and I swear he was up and down from the table twenty times before we even finished the turkey. He's always got some project going on. Last year he decided he was going to build a deck onto the back of their house. Completely by himself. He watched a bunch of YouTube videos and just bought out half of Home Depot. He got this massive miter saw—a 12-inch sliding compound one, beautiful piece of machinery. I helped him unload it from his truck. It's a Ford F-150, one of the newer ones with the aluminum body. They say those are supposed to get better gas mileage, but Dave complains about it all the time. He commutes about forty-five minutes each way, out past the new Amazon fulfillment center they just built off the interstate. That place is enormous, must be a million square feet. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't feel restless. And I'm not moving in slow motion or anything. I just... go about my day at my normal pace, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn’t worth living or had thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? No, no, I've never had thoughts like that. Never crossed my mind to, you know, end things. I mean, sure, I get this flat feeling, but I've got responsibilities. I've always taken my obligations seriously, right from when I was a kid. \n\nI remember my first real job, delivering the morning paper. The old Gazette, before they went digital and eventually got bought out by that conglomerate. I had this Schwinn ten-speed, fire engine red. Heavy as a tank, but it could take a beating. I'd be up at 4:30 AM every day, folding those papers and stuffing them into the canvas bags that hung over the handlebars. In the winter, the snow would get so deep the plows would leave these massive drifts at the end of every driveway. I had to wear these thick wool socks my grandmother knitted for me—itchy as all get out, but they kept my toes from freezing off. She used to knit all the time, sitting in this floral armchair that smelled like peppermint and mothballs. She made me this sweater once, bright orange. Said it was so the cars would see me in the dark. I looked like a giant traffic cone riding down Elm Street. \n\nBut, uh, anyway. No, I don't have any thoughts about hurting myself or anything. Just... just feeling kind of empty, like I've been saying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've completely lost interest in activities that used to matter a lot to you, like your work, your garden, or time with your family?\n\nPatient: Lost interest? Yeah... I suppose so. For a long time now, there's just been this... this gray, flat feeling most days. Even for the things I used to really look forward to, like tending the tomatoes out back. \n\nI used to grow these incredible Cherokee Purples. You have to stake them up real good because the fruit gets so heavy. I bought these heavy-duty galvanized steel cages from this local hardware store, Miller's. Old man Miller ran that place for forty years before he sold it to one of those big chains. It was a real shame. Miller's had these hardwood floors that creaked every time you took a step, and they sold nails by the pound out of these wooden barrels. You just don't see that anymore. Everything's wrapped in that thick plastic clamshell packaging now. You need a pair of tin snips just to open a pack of batteries. I bought a pair of tin snips last month, actually—Wiss brand, the ones with the yellow handles. Good quality steel. I needed them to fix a piece of aluminum flashing on the roof near the chimney. The wind caught it during that big thunderstorm we had a few weeks back. Blew half the neighborhood's trash cans right down the street. I saw a junior tech do that once back in, oh, must have been '98. Back when we were still running Novell NetWare. Brought in this consulting firm from Chicago—bunch of kids in expensive suits who didn't know a server blade from a toaster. They tried to implement this \"agile\" framework across the board. Sal had this old Ford F-150, must have had three hundred thousand miles on it. Most days, yeah.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I bought my first Tandy computer there. Saved up for six months to get it, and it only had, what, 4 kilobytes of RAM? You had to hook it up to a cassette recorder just to save your programs. It took twenty minutes to load a simple text game, and half the time the tape would jam anyway. I remember trying to fix one of those cassette decks with a Q-tip and some rubbing alcohol, and I ended up getting the cotton swab stuck right in the gears. \n\nBut, uh, no, my focus is fine. I can still troubleshoot a network issue faster than most of those young guys they hire. I don't have any trouble thinking clearly. Just... just that flat feeling, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you move or speak more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, that you can't sit still or feel restless most days?\n\nPatient: Move or speak slowly? No, I don't think so. I haven't noticed anything like that. And I'm not really the restless type, either. I've always been pretty even-keeled, you know? Never one to bounce my leg or pace around the room. \n\nActually, my brother-in-law, Dave, he's the restless one. The guy cannot sit still to save his life. We went over to their place for Thanksgiving last year, and I swear he was up and down from the table twenty times before we even finished the turkey. He's always got some project going on. Last year he decided he was going to build a deck onto the back of their house. Completely by himself. He watched a bunch of YouTube videos and just bought out half of Home Depot. He got this massive miter saw—a 12-inch sliding compound one, beautiful piece of machinery. I helped him unload it from his truck. It's a Ford F-150, one of the newer ones with the aluminum body. They say those are supposed to get better gas mileage, but Dave complains about it all the time. He commutes about forty-five minutes each way, out past the new Amazon fulfillment center they just built off the interstate. That place is enormous, must be a million square feet. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't feel restless. And I'm not moving in slow motion or anything. I just... go about my day at my normal pace, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn’t worth living or had thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? No, no, I've never had thoughts like that. Never crossed my mind to, you know, end things. I mean, sure, I get this flat feeling, but I've got responsibilities. I've always taken my obligations seriously, right from when I was a kid. \n\nI remember my first real job, delivering the morning paper. The old Gazette, before they went digital and eventually got bought out by that conglomerate. I had this Schwinn ten-speed, fire engine red. Heavy as a tank, but it could take a beating. I'd be up at 4:30 AM every day, folding those papers and stuffing them into the canvas bags that hung over the handlebars. In the winter, the snow would get so deep the plows would leave these massive drifts at the end of every driveway. I had to wear these thick wool socks my grandmother knitted for me—itchy as all get out, but they kept my toes from freezing off. She used to knit all the time, sitting in this floral armchair that smelled like peppermint and mothballs. She made me this sweater once, bright orange. Said it was so the cars would see me in the dark. I looked like a giant traffic cone riding down Elm Street. \n\nBut, uh, anyway. No, I don't have any thoughts about hurting myself or anything. Just... just feeling kind of empty, like I've been saying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've completely lost interest in activities that used to matter a lot to you, like your work, your garden, or time with your family?\n\nPatient: Lost interest? Yeah... I suppose so. For a long time now, there's just been this... this gray, flat feeling most days. Even for the things I used to really look forward to, like tending the tomatoes out back. \n\nI used to grow these incredible Cherokee Purples. You have to stake them up real good because the fruit gets so heavy. I bought these heavy-duty galvanized steel cages from this local hardware store, Miller's. Old man Miller ran that place for forty years before he sold it to one of those big chains. It was a real shame. Miller's had these hardwood floors that creaked every time you took a step, and they sold nails by the pound out of these wooden barrels. You just don't see that anymore. Everything's wrapped in that thick plastic clamshell packaging now. You need a pair of tin snips just to open a pack of batteries. I bought a pair of tin snips last month, actually—Wiss brand, the ones with the yellow handles. Good quality steel. I needed them to fix a piece of aluminum flashing on the roof near the chimney. The wind caught it during that big thunderstorm we had a few weeks back. Blew half the neighborhood's trash cans right down the street. I saw my neighbor's recycling bin rolling past my driveway like a tumbleweed. Took him three days to find the lid. \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Right, the tomatoes. Yeah, tending the garden, or going fishing, or even going to a church concert... they just don't bring me any joy anymore. They just feel like items on a checklist, you know? Just another thing I have to cross off before the day is over.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself having trouble making decisions, even about small things, most days?\n\nPatient: Trouble making decisions? No, not really. I mean, I don't get stuck trying to figure out what to have for breakfast or anything like that. I just make a choice and move on. \n\nIt's like when I was setting up the new server racks at work last month. You have to decide on the cable management routing pretty early on, or you end up with a rat's nest. I always go with the Velcro ties instead of zip ties. Zip ties are a nightmare if you ever have to swap out a patch cable. You end up trying to snip them with wire cutters and accidentally nicking the Cat6 casing. I saw a junior tech do that once back in, oh, must have been '98. Back when we were still running Novell NetWare. He took down the entire accounting department for three hours right at the end of the quarter. The CFO came down to the server room, face red as a beet. He was this short, stocky guy named Arthur. Wore these suspenders every single day, even in the dead of summer. Arthur had this vintage Mercedes he parked in the corner spot. A 1980 300SD, turbo diesel. Beautiful car, built like a tank. He was always talking about how he imported the replacement parts straight from Germany. Took weeks to get a water pump. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... decisions. No, I don't have trouble making them. I just decide what needs doing and do it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been down or flat for most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two years?\n\nPatient: Down or flat? Yeah... yeah, I'd say that's accurate. It's been a long time. This has been going on since I was pulled back into full operational leadership, which was... let's see, about two years and four months ago now. \n\nThey restructured the whole IT department. Brought in this consulting firm from Chicago—bunch of kids in expensive suits who didn't know a server blade from a toaster. They tried to implement this \"agile\" framework across the board. Complete disaster. We had perfectly good legacy systems running the inventory databases. I remember when we first installed those IBM mainframes back in the early nineties. Took a crane to get them into the building. We had to reinforce the raised flooring in the data center just to handle the weight. The contractor who did the flooring, Sal, he used to bring in these massive boxes of cannolis from a bakery in the North End. Real ricotta, none of that cheap filler. Sal had this old Ford F-150, must have had three hundred thousand miles on it. The tailgate was held shut with a bungee cord, and he had this golden retriever named Buster who rode shotgun everywhere he went. Buster would just sit there with his head out the window, ears flapping in the wind. \n\nBut, uh... yeah. Ever since that restructure, getting pulled back into the day-to-day operations... it's just been this constant, flat feeling. Most days, yeah."
  }
